      UNCONDITIONAL DETERMINISTIC SUBEXPONENTIAL INTEGER
       FACTORING AND QUADRATIC NONRESIDUE CONSTRUCTION

        Abstract. We establish three unconditional deterministic complexity bounds in algorithmic number
        theory, using zero randomness, zero unproven smoothness hypotheses, and zero Riemann-hypothesis
        assumptions, with effective absolute implied constants throughout:
        (1) Every composite integer N ≥ 4 can be factored deterministically on a multi-tape Turing machine
            (and on a standard word-RAM) in worst-case bit-operation complexity

         (2) Given any non-perfect-power composite integer N ≥ 4 and any even integer M  ˜︁ ≥ 2 divisible
             by p − 1 for every prime p | N , a nontrivial divisor of N can be found deterministically in
             worst-case bit complexity

        The splitting and nonresidue algorithms pull back the active prime-or-semiprime quadratic character
        along compatible modular square-root homomorphisms into 2K − 1 quadratic fields and their
        pairwise biquadratic composita, enumerate short principal ideals and units via four-dimensional
        weighted Lenstra–Lenstra–Lovász infrastructure walks, trivialize class-group cocycles so that twisted
        smooth ideal sums satisfy Walsh–Hadamard positivity, and contradict a uniform sixteenth-moment
        quadratic-field Burgess bound. The exponent-annihilation algorithm constructs preconditioned pairs
        of totally-real-targeted base-m polynomials, certifies dense joint squarefree smooth relations via
        Landau–Page zero-repulsion, Montgomery zero-density estimates, and stopping-time factor-base
        peeling, extracts prime-order multiples modulo N via two-field real-unit annihilation, and saturates
        subgroup indices across all prime factors of N .

                       1. Introduction, Main Results, and Notation
1.1. Background and Prior Complexity Barriers. A central open problem in computational
number theory is to determine the worst-case deterministic complexity of factoring a composite
integer N ≥ 4 given in binary without random coin tosses or unproven analytic hypotheses.
Whereas primality testing was shown to lie in deterministic polynomial time by Agrawal, Kayal, and
Saxena [1], and randomized integer factoring admits rigorous subexponential expected running time
LN [1/2, 1 + o(1)] via smooth-relation congruences of squares [39], every unconditional deterministic
general integer factoring algorithm known prior to the present work required exponential bit
complexity N c+o(1) for a strictly positive
                                       √    constant c > 0.
   Specifically, trial division up to ⌊ N ⌋ uses N 1/2+o(1) bit operations, improved to N 1/3+o(1) by
Lehman [37] and to N 1/4+o(1) by Pollard [44] and Strassen [53] via fast multipoint polynomial

=== PAGE 3 ===
          UNCONDITIONAL DETERMINISTIC SUBEXPONENTIAL FACTORING AND NONRESIDUES                              3


evaluation (with polynomial-space and logarithmic-factor refinements by Coppersmith [15], Bostan,
Gaudry, and Schost [6], Costa and Harvey [17], and Hittmeir [30]). After nearly five decades at
the exponent 1/4, Hittmeir [31] achieved N 2/9+o(1) , and Harvey [23] obtained N 1/5+o(1) , followed
by logarithmic-factor and sub-polynomial refinements at the same exponent 1/5 by Harvey and
Hittmeir [24, 25] and Stănică [52]. Even under the Extended or Generalized Riemann Hypothesis
(ERH/GRH), no deterministic subexponential (N o(1) ) general integer factoring algorithm was
previously known.
    Two distinct obstacles have stood in the way of unconditional deterministic subexponential integer
factoring:
  (i) Exponent annihilation modulo unknown prime divisors. In Pollard’s p − 1 method and related
      group-order methods, factoring reduces to Miller’s 2-adic order splitting once one knows an even
      integer M˜︂ ≥ 2 divisible by p − 1 for every prime p | N whose bit-length ln M   ˜︂ is subexponential
      in ln N . Deterministically constructing such an integer M      ˜︂ in subexponential time requires
      producing multiplicative relations modulo N without random sampling and without assuming
      unproven polynomial-smoothness heuristics.
 (ii) Order splitting and quadratic nonresidue construction without ERH. Even if an oracle supplies
      φ(N ), λ(N ), or an even common multiple M       ˜︂ of {p − 1 : p | N } (with ln M  ˜︂ possibly much
      larger than ln N , where lattice root-finding methods such as Coron and May [16] do not apply),
      Miller’s deterministic reduction [40] splits a non-perfect-power composite N (︁only  )︁ when supplied
                                                                                         z
      with an integer z on which an active primitive quadratic character χ(z) = q (q | N prime or
      semiprime) takes the value −1. Under ERH, Ankeny [2] (made explicit by Bach [3]) showed that
      the least positive integer with χ(z) = −1 is O((ln q)2 ), giving a conditional polynomial-time
      reduction (studied in unconditional special cases and multi-query oracle settings by Woll [57],
      Landau [36], Żrałek [58, 59], Hittmeir and Pomykała [32], Morain, Renault, and Smith [42], and
      Dąbrowski, Pomykała, and Shparlinski [18]). Unconditionally, however, the sharpest worst-case
      bound for finding a quadratic nonresidue modulo an odd             prime p—or a splitting witness
                                                                     (︁ 1/(4√e) )︁ 0.151632...+o(1)
      modulo N given M —remained the exponential bound Õ p
                          ˜︂                                                      (p                ) obtained
      by combining Vinogradov’s multiplicativity trick [56] with Burgess’s character-sum estimate
      [9, 10, 11, 12] (see also Hildebrand [26], Graham and Ringrose [21], and Banks and Guo [4]).
      Algebraic, elliptic-curve, and finite-field polynomial-factoring methods—including Schoof [49],
      Pila [43], Rónyai [46, 47], Shoup [50], Huang [33], Evdokimov [20], Sze [54], Ivanyos, Karpinski,
      Rónyai, and Saxena [34], Polymath [45], Bourgain, Konyagin, and Shparlinski [7], Bhargava,
      Ivanyos, Mittal, and Saxena [5], Chinburg and Ding [14], and Spaenlehauer [51]—either require
      GRH, restrict the arithmetic structure of p − 1, assume a nonresidue in Fp as advice, or incur
      complexity at least p1/2+o(1) in the worst case.
    Notice that solving (i) alone while retaining   the classical Vinogradov–Burgess scan for (ii) would
                                          (︁ 1/(4√e)+ε )︁
stall at worst-case bit complexity Oε N                   . In this monograph we resolve both obstacles
unconditionally in deterministic subexponential time.

1.2. Statements of the Main Results. Recall that for a real variable x ≥ 4 and α ∈ (0, 1), the
subexponential complexity notation Lx [α, o(1)] denotes the class of functions T (x) satisfying
                                                 ln max(T (x), 1)
                                     lim sup                        ≤ 0.
                                      x→∞      (ln x)α (ln ln x)1−α
An integer N ≥ 4 is composite if it is not prime, and is a perfect
                                                            (︁ z )︁ power if N = xk for some integers
x ≥ 2 and k ≥ 2. For an odd prime p and an integer z, p ∈ {−1, 0, +1} denotes the Legendre
symbol modulo p. All algorithms below are explicit, parameter-free, purely deterministic algorithms
analyzed in the elementary bit-operation model, in which comparisons, parity and bit tests, binary
conversions, additions, and subtractions on b-bit binary integers take O(b + 1) bit operations, k-bit

=== PAGE 4 ===
4          UNCONDITIONAL DETERMINISTIC SUBEXPONENTIAL FACTORING AND NONRESIDUES


shifts take O(b + k + 1) bit operations, and schoolbook multiplication, Euclidean division with
remainder, and Euclidean/extended greatest common divisor take O(b2 ) bit operations (charging
at least one unit per loop or recursion step). Because elementary bit operations execute within a
constant factor on a deterministic multi-tape Turing machine and on a standard logarithmic-cost
word-RAM—without requiring fast integer multiplication—every worst-case bit-operation bound
stated below holds identically in all three models. Note also that Afactor handles prime powers
directly via elementary root extraction and never invokes polynomial-time primality testing. All
implied constants in O(·) bounds are effective and absolute.
Theorem 1.1 (Unconditional Deterministic Subexponential General Integer Factoring). There
exist an explicit, parameter-free, purely deterministic, unconditional algorithm Afactor , an effective
absolute constant Cfac > 0, and a function c : N → R satisfying limN →∞ c(N ) = 0 such that, given
any composite integer N ≥ 4 encoded in binary, Afactor (N ) always halts and outputs a verified
nontrivial divisor d of N satisfying 1 < d < N in worst-case bit-operation cost Tfactor (N ) satisfying
both

    Tfactor (N ) ≤ Cfac exp 3(ln N )3/4                               Tfactor (N ) ≤ exp c(N ) (ln N )3/4 (ln ln N )1/4 ,

1.3. Overview of the Proofs. The monograph is organized into two self-contained parts that
prove Theorem 1.2 and Theorem 1.3 independently from classical foundations, followed by the short
synthesis proving Theorem 1.1.
Part I (Sections 2–6:   √
                           Proof of Theorem 1.2). Why does Vinogradov’s classical trick stall at
                    1/(4  e) for a quadratic character χ(z) = zq (q prime or a product of two odd
                                                             (︁ )︁
exponential scale q
primes)? If χ(a) = +1 for all positive integers a ≤ Y , then χ(n) = +1 on all Y -smooth integers

n ≤ X, where X = q 1/3 lies inside the Burgess range; however, √        integers n ≤ X having a prime
factor r > Y can carry χ(n) = −1, and once X = Y u with u > e, those uncontrolled negative
contributions outweigh the smooth integers. Crucially, we do not attempt to bound the least positive
rational integer nonresidue (which is controlled by zeros of L(s, χ)); instead, we construct algebraic
integers across a multiquadratic family of number fields whose reductions modulo N (or modulo p)
must include an element with χ = −1.
    Given (N, M˜︂) (with M ˜︂ = p − 1 when N = p is prime), let q0 := −1 and let q1 < · · · < qK be the
first K rational primes (K ≍ (ln q)1/2 ). Running Miller’s 2-Sylow order test on q0 , q1 , . . . , qK either
splits N immediately or exhibits a maximal 2-power order element ζ ≡ qju∗odd (mod N ) generating all
K + 1 projections modulo N . Pairing the remaining K elements against odd powers of qj∗ extracts
K squarefree integers d(1) , . . . , d(K) and compatible modular square roots (dℓ , sℓ ) for all 2K√− 1

that agree pairwise on every biquadratic compositum Eℓ1 ,ℓ2 = Q( dℓ1 , dℓ2 ) (Section 3).
    In Section 4, we give a polynomial-time (in field discriminant, norm bound, and log N ) deter-
ministic algorithm that enumerates modular images of unit-group generators and short coprime
principal-ideal generators in every imaginary/real quadratic field Fℓ (via Gauss reduction cycles)
and every totally real or CM biquadratic field Eℓ1 ,ℓ2 (by computing relative norms to real qua-
dratic subfields to pin down the Archimedean log-modulus target up to at most 8 unit-parity
bits, then walking via fixed-point four-dimensional weighted LLL on dual ideals—replacing general
higher-degree regulator search [8]—while tracking modular images in (Z/N Z)∗ without coefficient
blowup).
    In Section 5, we show that if χ = +1 holds on [1, Y ] and on all enumerated units and short
principal-ideal  generators of Fℓ and Eℓ1 ,ℓ2 , then Minkowski class representatives of norm at most

1.4. General Notation and Arithmetic Conventions. Throughout the monograph, we adopt
the following standard notation:
    • Modular residues and greatest common divisors. For z ∈ Z and M ∈ Z≥1 , write
      z mod M for the unique integer in {0, . . . , M − 1} congruent to z modulo M , and define
      gcd(0, M ) := M . Write F2 = {0, 1} for the field of two elements; for ℓ = (ℓ1 , . . . , ℓK ) and
                                                    ∑︁K
      v = (v1 , . . . , vK ) in FK
                                 2 , write ℓ · v :=
                                                                                0                  1
                                                      r=1 ℓr vr ∈ F2 , with (−1) := +1 and (−1) := −1.
                                                                       +          −
    • Arithmetic functions. For an integer n ≥ 1, let P (n) and P (n) denote the largest and
      smallest prime factors of n, respectively, with the conventions           P + (1) := 1 and P − (1) := +∞.
      Write supp(n) := {p prime : p | n}, ω(n) := | supp(n)| = p|n 1, τ (n) := d|n 1, φ(n) :=
                                                                             ∑︁                ∑︁

      #(Z/nZ)∗ for Euler’s totient function, µ(n) for the Möbius function, and Λ(n) for the von
      Mangoldt function (Λ(pk ) := ln p for prime p and k ∈ Z≥1 , and Λ(n) := 0 otherwise). For real
      x, write π(x) := #{p ≤ x : p prime}. For a rational prime p, write vp : Q∗ → Z for the p-adic
      valuation, with vp (0) := +∞. Given a set of primes J, an integer n ≥ 1 is called squarefree
      J-smooth if n is squarefree and supp(n) ⊆ J (including n = 1).
    • Norms and polynomials. For a vector v = (v1 , . . . , vm )T ∈ Rm , write ∥v∥1 := m                 j=1 |vj |,

    • Asymptotic and bit-complexity conventions. Implied constants in O(·), (︁≪, ≫, and ≍ are)︁
      effective and absolute unless subscripted by parameters, and Õ(T ) stands for O T (ln(T +3))O(1)
      with effective absolute implied constants. For n ∈ Z, write bitLen(n) := ⌊log2 |n|⌋ + 1 if
      n ̸= 0 and bitLen(0) := 0. Throughout our algorithmic analyses, all integer arithmetic uses
      deterministic schoolbook algorithms without fast multiplication: on integers of bit-length at
      most b, addition, subtraction, negation, comparison, parity/bit-testing, and binary bit-list

=== PAGE 7 ===
                UNCONDITIONAL DETERMINISTIC SUBEXPONENTIAL FACTORING AND NONRESIDUES                       7


    conversion take O(b + 1) bit operations, k-bit shifts take O(b + k + 1) bit operations, and
    multiplication, Euclidean division with remainder, and Euclidean/extended greatest common
    divisor take O(b2 ) bit operations.
  • Algebraic number fields. For an algebraic number field F of finite degree [F : Q], let
    OF denote its ring of integers, OF∗ its unit group, µ(F ) its√︁  finite subgroup of roots of unity,
    ∆F := disc(OF ) ∈ Z \ {0} its absolute discriminant, MF := |∆F |, NF/Q and TrF/Q its norm
    and trace (NF/Q (a) = [OF : a] for nonzero integral ideals a ⊂ OF ), and Cl(F ) its finite abelian
    ideal class group, writing [a] ∈ Cl(F ) for the class of a. Since OF is a Dedekind domain, every
    nonzero integral ideal factors uniquely into maximal ideals, NF/Q is completely multiplicative on
    nonzero integral ideals, and NF/Q (γOF ) = |NF/Q (γ)| for γ ∈ OF \ {0}. For each maximal ideal
    p ⊂ OF above a rational prime p (written p | p), write vp : F ∗ → Z for the normalized p-adic
    valuation (vp (0) := +∞) and e(p/p), f (p/p) for the ramification index and residue degree.

(1) Let Λ ⊂ Rm (m ≥ 1) be a full-rank lattice with Z-basis (v1 , . . . , vm ) and covolume V :=

2.4. Elementary Chebyshev, Primorial, and Smooth-Number Bounds.
Lemma 2.3 (Elementary Chebyshev, Primorial, and Smooth-Number Bounds).
 (1) For every real x ≥ 1, p≤x prime p ≤ 4x . For every real Y ≥ 4096, π(Y ) − π(Y /2) > 12 Yln Y .

  3. Deterministic Miller Order Testing and Compatible Multiquadratic Square
                                      Roots
  We now formalize Miller’s deterministic 2-adic order test, identify the active prime-or-semiprime
quadratic splitting character when N has at least two distinct odd prime divisors, and show that if
none of q0 = −1, q1 = 2, . . . , qK splits N , then one can deterministically extract compatible modular
square roots across 2K − 1 distinct squarefree radicands dividing PK .

      4. Deterministic Unit and Principal-Ideal Enumeration in Quadratic and
                                 Biquadratic Fields
  To certify cocycle-free ideal-character extensions in Section 5, we need deterministic algorithms
that enumerate the modular images in (Z/N Z)∗ of generating sets of units and generators of all
coprime principal ideals of bounded norm in both quadratic and biquadratic number fields, without
ever writing down coefficient-exploded algebraic integers. We first record the exact four-dimensional
case of the deterministic lattice-basis reduction theorem of Lenstra, Lenstra, and Lovász [38].
Proposition 4.1 (Deterministic LLL Basis Reduction in Z4 ; Lenstra–Lenstra–Lovász [38, Props. 1.6,
1.9, and 1.26]). Let M   ˆ︂ ∈ Z4×4 be a nonsingular integer matrix whose columns have Euclidean
norms bounded by Bin ≥ 2. Running the deterministic δ = 34 Lenstra–Lenstra–Lovász basis-
reduction algorithm (maintaining exact integer Bareiss Gram–Schmidt minors) on the columns of M             ˆ︂
halts in O (log Bin )3 bit operations and outputs a Z-basis (b1 , b2 , b3 , b4 ) of M     ˆ︂Z4 together with a
          (︁          )︁

unimodular matrix U ∈ Z      4×4  (| det U | = 1) satisfying M
                                                             ˆ︂U = (b1 , b2 , b3 , b4 ), whose Gram–Schmidt
                                                    ∑︁j−1
orthogonalization (b1 , b2 , b3 , b4 ) (bj = bj + i=1 µji b∗i ) satisfies
                      ∗    ∗   ∗   ∗            ∗

Lemma 4.2 (Deterministic Quadratic and Biquadratic Unit and Principal-Ideal Enumeration).
                                                                              √
(1) (Quadratic fields.) Let d ∈ Z \ {1} be squarefree, put F := Q( d) and D := |∆F | ≤ 4|d|, let
    Z ≥ 1 be an integer, let N ≥ 3 be an odd integer with gcd(d, N ) = 1, and let s ∈ (Z/N Z)∗
    satisfy s2 ≡ d (mod N ).
    (a) Assume N > max(D, Z). There is an explicit deterministic multi-tape Turing-machine
         algorithm running in poly(D, Z, log N ) bit operations that either outputs a nontrivial divisor
         1 < g < N of N , or outputs a finite subset UF ⊆ (Z/N Z)∗ containing φF,N (u) for every
         u in a generating set of OF∗ , and containing, for every principal ideal a ∈ PF+ (N ) with
         1 ≤ NF/Q (a) ≤ Z, the residue φF,N (γ) for at least one generator γ of a.
                                                           √
     (b) Suppose d > 0 and assume N > max(⌊ D⌋, Z). Then OF∗ = {±εnd : n ∈ Z} for a unique
         fundamental unit εd ∈ OF∗ with σF,1 (εd ) > 1, satisfying 25 < ln σF,1 (εd ) < 21 D ln D. Every
         nonzero integral ideal a √      ⊂ OF factors uniquely as m0 I(a, b) with m0 ∈ Z≥1 and a primitive
                                      b+ D

         either outputs a divisor 1 < g < N , certifies that I(ain , bin ) is non-principal, or outputs

     5. Cocycle-Free Ideal-Character Extension and Walsh–Hadamard Positivity
  We next prove that if a prime-or-semiprime quadratic character χ(z) = zq equals +1 on [1, Y ]
                                                                                 (︁ )︁

and on all units and short principal-ideal generators across the multiquadratic family (Fℓ )ℓ∈FK \{0}
                                                                                                2
and its pairwise biquadratic composita Eℓ1 ,ℓ2 , then χ ◦ φF,q extends without class-group cocycles to
completely multiplicative ideal characters whose norm sums have nonnegative Walsh–Hadamard
coefficients at every positive integer coprime to PK q.

             6. Uniform Quadratic-Field Burgess Bound and Proof of Theorem 1.2
6.1. Planar Lattice Point Counting and Packing.
Lemma 6.1 (Planar Lattice Counts). Let Λ ⊂ R2 be a full-rank lattice of covolume V > 0
satisfying ∥w∥∞ ≥ µ > 0 for all w ∈ Λ \ {0}. Set ρ := 2V           µ . For every axis-aligned rectangle
               2
R = I1 × I2 ⊂ R whose side intervals I1 , I2 (open, half-open, or closed) have lengths S1 , S2 > 0, we
have #(Λ ∩ R) ≤ (S1 +2ρ)(S
                       V
                          2 +2ρ)
                                 , and if S1 , S2 > 2ρ then #(Λ ∩ R) ≥ (S1 −2ρ)(S
                                                                                V
                                                                                  2 −2ρ)
                                                                                         . Moreover, for
            2
                            (︁                    2
                                                    )︁ (︁ 2S
                                                              )︁ 2
every z0 ∈ R and S > 0, # Λ ∩ (z0 + [−S, S] ) ≤ µ + 1 .

Proof. Choose w1 ∈ Λ \ {0} minimizing ∥w1 ∥2 (so ∥w1 ∥2 ≥ µ), and choose w2 ∈ Λ \ Zw1 minimizing
the perpendicular distance h > 0 to Rw1 , shifted by an integer multiple of w1 so that |⟨w2 , w1 ⟩| ≤
1      2                                                                   2        2   1      2    2
2 ∥w1 ∥2 ; then (w1 , w2 ) is a Z-basis of Λ with h = V /∥w1 ∥2 , and ∥w1 ∥2 ≤ ∥w2 ∥2 ≤ 4 ∥w1 ∥2 + h ≤
1      2     2                            2   V     2V                                            1 1
4 ∥w2 ∥2 + h gives ∥w1 ∥2 ≤ ∥w2 ∥2 ≤ 3 ∥w1 ∥2 < µ = ρ. Thus P := {t1 w1 + t2 w2 : t1 , t2 ∈ [− 2 , 2 )}
                                         √

has area V , tiles R2 via Λ, and satisfies ∥u∥∞ ≤ ∥u∥2 ≤ 12 (∥w1 ∥2 + ∥w2 ∥2 ) < ρ for all u ∈ P . Disjoint
translates w + P (w ∈ Λ ∩ R) lie in the ρ-thickening of R of area (S1 + 2ρ)(S2 + 2ρ), and when
S1 , S2 > 2ρ the open ρ-shrinking of R of area (S1 − 2ρ)(S2 − 2ρ) is covered by {w + P : w ∈ Λ ∩ R},
giving both rectangle bounds. Finally, the open squares w + (− µ2 , µ2 )2 (w ∈ Λ ∩ (z0 + [−S, S]2 )) are
pairwise disjoint inside z0 + (−S − µ2 , S + µ2 )2 , yielding the packing bound.                         □

6.2. Uniform Sixteenth-Moment Quadratic-Field Burgess Bound. Whereas Hinz [27, 28,
29] proved Burgess bounds for primitive characters in a fixed number field K with unspecified
discriminant dependence ≪K,r,ε , our contradiction requires an effective bound across varying
imaginary and real quadratic fields Fℓ with explicit polynomial dependence on Dℓ = |∆Fℓ |, valid
uniformly for both prime moduli and semiprime moduli q = p1 p2 even when p1 > Dℓ is arbitrarily
small. We derive this directly from Schmidt’s elementary proof of Weil’s character-sum bound over
Fp .

form c g(X)2 for any c ∈ Fp and g(X) ∈ Fp [X]. Then

     By Euler’s criterion (proved in Lemma 3.1(2)), every output lies in {1, . . . , p − 1} and satisfies

(while 3 ≤ p < pthr costs O(1)), giving an effective constant C2∗ > 0. Setting Csplit := max(C1∗ , C2∗ )
completes the proof of Theorem 1.2.                                                                 □

Part 2. Preconditioned Base-m Smooth Relations, Two-Field Real-Unit Annihilation,
        and Deterministic (p − 1)-Exponent Annihilation
     7. Classical Analytic Inputs, Non-Monic Valuations, and Two-Field Order
                                     Extraction
   Throughout Part 2 (Sections 7–11), for an integer N ≥ 16 we write L := ln N , L2 := ln ln N , and
κ := ln 2 = 0.693147 . . . , and f = o(g) means |f |/g → 0 effectively as N → ∞. For any Q-irreducible
polynomial f ∈ Z[X] of degree d ≥ 2, write Kf := Q[X]/(f (X)) = Q(θf ) with θf := X mod f (X).

7.1. Classical Analytic Inputs and Complex/Continued-Fraction Lemmas. We record the
three effective classical theorems from analytic number theory used in Sections 8–11, together with
the Borel–Carathéodory inequality, truncated Perron estimate, and Legendre continued-fraction
criterion. Note that Proposition 7.1(2) uses only the effective Landau–Page theorem and never
invokes Siegel’s ineffective bound on real zeros.

7.4. Certified Deterministic Order-Extraction Algorithm TryPair.
Lemma 7.5 (Deterministic Order Extractor TryPair). Let N ≥ 9, m ≥ 2, d ≥ 9, cd ≥ 1, A ≥ 2,
B ≥ 4, K1 ≥ 16, and L0 ≥ 10 be integers with N ≤ 2dL0 , gcd(cd , N ) = 1, and m, A, B, K1 ≤ 2L0 ,

and let f1 , f2 ∈ Z[X] be Q-irreducible degree-d polynomials with common leading coefficient cd and
cont(f1 ) = cont(f2 ) = 1 satisfying f1 (m) ≡ f2 (m) ≡ 0 (mod N ) and ∥f1 ∥∞ , ∥f2 ∥∞ ≤ 2L0 . Define

11.3. Synthesis: Proof of Theorem 1.1.

Proof of Theorem 1.1. Define Afactor (N ) on any composite integer N ≥ 4 encoded in binary (put
bN := ⌊log2 N ⌋ + 1 ≥ 3):
  1. If 2 | N , output 2 and halt (valid since N ≥ 4 implies 1 < 2 < N ).

=== PAGE 57 ===
            UNCONDITIONAL DETERMINISTIC SUBEXPONENTIAL FACTORING AND NONRESIDUES                                       57


  2. For each integer k = 2, 3, . . . , bN , binary-search [2, 2⌈bN /k⌉ ] using clamped k-th powering
     (clamping intermediate products at N + 1) to test whether xk = N for some integer x ≥ 2; if so,
     output x and halt (valid since N is composite and k ≥ 2 give 1 < x ≤ N 1/2 < N and x | N ).
  3. Now N is an odd composite integer (N ≥ 9) and not a perfect power (as N = xk with x, k ≥ 2
     forces 2k ≤ N < 2bN , hence 2 ≤ k ≤ bN and x ≤ 2⌈bN /k⌉ ). Run Aexp (N ) from Theorem 1.3. If
     it returns a divisor candidate d′ , verify 1 < d′ < N and N mod d′ = 0 and output d′ ; otherwise
     it outputs an even integer M ˜︂ ≥ 2 satisfying (p − 1) | M ˜︂ for every prime divisor p of N and
        ˜︂ ≤ exp (ln N )0.65 .
                 (︁         )︁
     ln M
  4. Run Asplit (N, M˜︂) from Theorem 1.2(a) and output the returned nontrivial divisor 1 < d < N
     of N .
